# Array slicing and extraction plan

## Recommendation

Use ordinary R subscripts for slicing and extraction:

```r
rray_slice(x, ...)
rray_slice_assign(x, ..., value)

rray_slice_axis(x, i, ..., axis)
rray_slice_assign_axis(x, i, ..., axis, value)

rray_slice_rows(x, i)
rray_slice_assign_rows(x, i, value)

rray_slice_columns(x, i)
rray_slice_assign_columns(x, i, value)

rray_extract(x, i)
rray_extract_assign(x, i, value)
```

`rray_slice()` performs dimension-preserving orthogonal selection across one
or more axes. It requires exactly one subscript per source axis, so `TRUE`
selects a whole axis. `rray_slice_axis()` performs the same operation on one
axis held in a variable, and `rray_slice_rows()` and `rray_slice_columns()`
are its first and second axis shortcuts. `rray_extract()` accepts flat
positions or coordinate points and always returns a one-dimensional array.

Writing plans/wrap.md now.

Write plans/wrap.md 
# Making `r_wrap()` sound

This plan fixes a memory safety bug in our ALTREP wrapper (`src/wrapper.c`),
and makes sure the fix does not cost rray a copy of its inputs on every call.

The work lands as three stacked pull requests. Each one is green and does not
regress performance on its own.

1. `r_unwrap()`: add the complement to `r_wrap()`, and make every data read in
   `src/` go through it.

2. Lock the wrapper: port base R's fix, so a data pointer, once handed out,
   stays valid for as long as the wrapper is reachable.

3. Owned results: stop wrapping arrays that rray itself just allocated, in
   `rray_unstack()` and `rray_stack()`.

Read the whole document before starting. Part 1 explains the bug, Part 2 the
design, and Parts 3 to 5 list every change, pull request by pull request.

---

# Part 1: The bug

## How the wrapper works today

`r_wrap(x)` returns an ALTREP object whose `data1` slot holds the vector being
wrapped. rray uses it to change attributes (dimensions, names, titles) without
copying the data.

When something asks the wrapper for a data pointer, `src/wrapper.c` does this:

- Read-only request (`REAL_RO()`, `r_dbl_cbegin()`): return a pointer straight
  into `data1`.

- Writable request (`REAL()`, `r_dbl_begin()`): if `data1` might be shared,
  copy it, put the copy in `data1`, and return a pointer into the copy.

## What goes wrong

A read-only pointer taken before a writable request points into the old
`data1`, which the wrapper no longer holds.

```c
const double* p = REAL_RO(w);
double* q = REAL(w);
q[0] = 99;
// p[0] == 1, q[0] == 99, w[1] == 99
```

That pointer is stale at best. It can also dangle. Refcounts in R are only an
upper bound: they go down when a slot is overwritten, but not when the object
holding the reference is garbage collected. So the old `data1` can look shared
while nothing reachable holds it:

```r
x <- as.double(1:1e6)
l <- list(x)
w <- wrap(x)
rm(x, l)
```

After the swap, nothing reaches the old vector, and the next GC frees it. With
`MallocScribble=1` on macOS, reading `p[0]` after a GC returns `1.194531e+103`,
which is the `0x5555...` fill pattern of freed memory.

## Base R had the same bug

Base R's own wrapper class (`src/main/altclasses.c`) had exactly this pattern.
Ivan Krylov reported it, and Luke Tierney fixed it in three commits:

- `dbc99e13e8` (r90261, also in R-patched as r90260): the read-only `Dataptr`
  and `Dataptr_or_null` methods now copy shared data too, so a later writable
  request returns the same address.

- `c40bda6593` (r90309, R-devel): once any data pointer has been handed out,
  the wrapper is locked and its data is never replaced again. A shallow
  duplicate of a locked wrapper copies the data instead of sharing it.

- `3580627fa3` (r90315): handles `shallow_duplicate()` returning an object
  with a refcount above zero. This does not apply to us, because
  `r_clone_data()` always allocates a fresh vector.

---

# Part 2: Design

## The lock

The rule, taken from base R:

> No data pointer leaves the wrapper unless the wrapper is the only owner of
> its data. After that, the wrapper's data never changes.

Concretely:

- The first time any data pointer is requested (read-only or writable), the
  wrapper copies its data if the data is shared, then locks itself.

- A locked wrapper never replaces `data1`. Its data has a refcount of exactly
  1, because nothing in rray ever shares locked data (see the next two
  points).

- `r_wrap()` on a locked wrapper copies the data instead of sharing it.

- A shallow duplicate goes through `r_wrap()`, so it copies too.

The lock lives in `data2`, which we do not use today: `r_null` means unlocked,
`r_true` means locked.

`Dataptr_or_null` returns `NULL` for an unlocked wrapper and a pointer for a
locked one. It never copies and never locks. R falls back to `Elt` or
`Get_region` when it gets `NULL`, and we implement both. This differs from
base R, which copies here. `Dataptr_or_null` is meant to be cheap, and R calls
it often (for example in `ITERATE_BY_REGION`), so a copy there would make
plain reads expensive.

## The cost, and `r_unwrap()`

With the lock alone, every official read of a wrapper with shared data costs a
full copy. For rray that would be nearly every call, because `vec_as_array()`
(in `src/utils.c`) wraps every plain vector input, and the user's vector is
still bound in R:

```r
x <- as.double(1:1e6)
rray_sum(x)  # vec_as_array() wraps x, the sum reads it: full copy
```

So inside rray we never read data through the wrapper. We read it from the
vector underneath:

```c
r_obj* x_data = KEEP(r_unwrap(x));
const double* v_x = r_dbl_cbegin(x_data);
```

`r_unwrap(x)` returns `data1` for one of our wrappers and `x` for anything
else. It never copies and never locks.

Why this is safe:

- `x_data` is on our protect stack, so it stays alive even if something swaps
  the wrapper's `data1` while we read. The worst case is a stale view, and
  only if someone writes to `x` through the wrapper while we are reading.
  rray never writes to its inputs.

- `KEEP()` does not change refcounts, so borrowing never makes data look
  shared.

Outside rray, other packages and base R go through the official API. They pay
at most one copy per wrapper, and then get a pointer that stays valid.

## Rules for `x_data`

These hold everywhere in `src/`.

1. Name the unwrapped object `<name>_data`: `x_data`, `y_data`,
   `value_data`, `index_data`, `n_data`, `true_data`, and so on. Never
   `data`.

2. Always `KEEP()` the result of `r_unwrap()`, even when it looks reachable
   through `x`.

3. Read only data from `x_data`: `r_*_cbegin()`, `r_length()`,
   `r_typeof()`. Never read attributes from it. The wrapped vector still
   carries its own original attributes, which can be out of date. Dimensions
   and names always come from `x`.

4. Never store `x_data` in an R object: never return it, never
   `r_list_poke()` it, never set it as an attribute. That would raise its
   refcount for good (refcounts do not go down when a list is garbage
   collected), and a locked wrapper's data must keep a refcount of 1.

5. Never write through `x_data`. Only `r_*_cbegin()` on it, never
   `r_*_begin()`.

## What gets unwrapped

Every `r_<type>_cbegin()` on an object that is often our own wrapper, or that
can be as long as an array input:

- Array payloads: `x`, `y`, `condition`, `true_`, `false_`, `missing`,
  `value`, the elements of `xs`.

- Subscripts and index arrays.

- `n` in `rray_roll_each()`, which goes through `rray_cast()` and
  `rray_broadcast()`.

Out of scope, with the reason:

- Dimensions, axes, and small integer arguments (`times` and `n` in
  `rray_rep()` and `rray_roll()`, `times` in `rray_rep_each()`, `dimensions`
  in `rray_split()`). These go through `arg_as_bare_integer()` and friends,
  which never wrap, so they are only wrappers if a user passes an rray result
  as the argument. Even then the cost is one copy of a short vector, after
  which that wrapper is locked and reads are free.

- Names and titles (`r_list_cbegin(x_names)`, `r_chr_cbegin(axis_names)`).

Read plans/wrap.md 
1	# Making `r_wrap()` sound
2	
3	This plan fixes a memory safety bug in our ALTREP wrapper (`src/wrapper.c`),
4	and makes sure the fix does not cost rray a copy of its inputs on every call.
5	
6	The work lands as three stacked pull requests. Each one is green and does not
7	regress performance on its own.
8	
9	1. `r_unwrap()`: add the complement to `r_wrap()`, and make every data read in
10	   `src/` go through it.
11	
12	2. Lock the wrapper: port base R's fix, so a data pointer, once handed out,
13	   stays valid for as long as the wrapper is reachable.
14	
15	3. Owned results: stop wrapping arrays that rray itself just allocated, in
16	   `rray_unstack()` and `rray_stack()`.
17	
18	Read the whole document before starting. Part 1 explains the bug, Part 2 the
19	design, and Parts 3 to 5 list every change, pull request by pull request.
20	
21	---
22	
23	# Part 1: The bug
24	
25	## How the wrapper works today
26	
27	`r_wrap(x)` returns an ALTREP object whose `data1` slot holds the vector being
28	wrapped. rray uses it to change attributes (dimensions, names, titles) without
29	copying the data.
30	
31	When something asks the wrapper for a data pointer, `src/wrapper.c` does this:
32	
33	- Read-only request (`REAL_RO()`, `r_dbl_cbegin()`): return a pointer straight
34	  into `data1`.
35	
36	- Writable request (`REAL()`, `r_dbl_begin()`): if `data1` might be shared,
37	  copy it, put the copy in `data1`, and return a pointer into the copy.
38	
39	## What goes wrong
40	
41	A read-only pointer taken before a writable request points into the old
42	`data1`, which the wrapper no longer holds.
43	
44	```c
45	const double* p = REAL_RO(w);
46	double* q = REAL(w);
47	q[0] = 99;
48	// p[0] == 1, q[0] == 99, w[1] == 99
49	```
50	
51	That pointer is stale at best. It can also dangle. Refcounts in R are only an
52	upper bound: they go down when a slot is overwritten, but not when the object
53	holding the reference is garbage collected. So the old `data1` can look shared
54	while nothing reachable holds it:
55	
56	```r
57	x <- as.double(1:1e6)
58	l <- list(x)
59	w <- wrap(x)
60	rm(x, l)
61	```
62	
63	After the swap, nothing reaches the old vector, and the next GC frees it. With
64	`MallocScribble=1` on macOS, reading `p[0]` after a GC returns `1.194531e+103`,
65	which is the `0x5555...` fill pattern of freed memory.
66	
67	## Base R had the same bug
68	
69	Base R's own wrapper class (`src/main/altclasses.c`) had exactly this pattern.
70	Ivan Krylov reported it, and Luke Tierney fixed it in three commits:
71	
72	- `dbc99e13e8` (r90261, also in R-patched as r90260): the read-only `Dataptr`
73	  and `Dataptr_or_null` methods now copy shared data too, so a later writable
74	  request returns the same address.
75	
76	- `c40bda6593` (r90309, R-devel): once any data pointer has been handed out,
77	  the wrapper is locked and its data is never replaced again. A shallow
78	  duplicate of a locked wrapper copies the data instead of sharing it.
79	
80	- `3580627fa3` (r90315): handles `shallow_duplicate()` returning an object
81	  with a refcount above zero. This does not apply to us, because
82	  `r_clone_data()` always allocates a fresh vector.
83	
84	---
85	
86	# Part 2: Design
87	
88	## The lock
89	
90	The rule, taken from base R:
91	
92	> No data pointer leaves the wrapper unless the wrapper is the only owner of
93	> its data. After that, the wrapper's data never changes.
94	
95	Concretely:
96	
97	- The first time any data pointer is requested (read-only or writable), the
98	  wrapper copies its data if the data is shared, then locks itself.
99	
100	- A locked wrapper never replaces `data1`. Its data has a refcount of exactly
101	  1, because nothing in rray ever shares locked data (see the next two
102	  points).
103	
104	- `r_wrap()` on a locked wrapper copies the data instead of sharing it.
105	
106	- A shallow duplicate goes through `r_wrap()`, so it copies too.
107	
108	The lock lives in `data2`, which we do not use today: `r_null` means unlocked,
109	`r_true` means locked.
110	
111	`Dataptr_or_null` returns `NULL` for an unlocked wrapper and a pointer for a
112	locked one. It never copies and never locks. R falls back to `Elt` or
113	`Get_region` when it gets `NULL`, and we implement both. This differs from
114	base R, which copies here. `Dataptr_or_null` is meant to be cheap, and R calls
115	it often (for example in `ITERATE_BY_REGION`), so a copy there would make
116	plain reads expensive.
117	
118	## The cost, and `r_unwrap()`
119	
120	With the lock alone, every official read of a wrapper with shared data costs a
121	full copy. For rray that would be nearly every call, because `vec_as_array()`
122	(in `src/utils.c`) wraps every plain vector input, and the user's vector is
123	still bound in R:
124	
125	```r
126	x <- as.double(1:1e6)
127	rray_sum(x)  # vec_as_array() wraps x, the sum reads it: full copy
128	```
129	
130	So inside rray we never read data through the wrapper. We read it from the
131	vector underneath:
132	
133	```c
134	r_obj* x_data = KEEP(r_unwrap(x));
135	const double* v_x = r_dbl_cbegin(x_data);
136	```
137	
138	`r_unwrap(x)` returns `data1` for one of our wrappers and `x` for anything
139	else. It never copies and never locks.
140	
141	Why this is safe:
142	
143	- `x_data` is on our protect stack, so it stays alive even if something swaps
144	  the wrapper's `data1` while we read. The worst case is a stale view, and
145	  only if someone writes to `x` through the wrapper while we are reading.
146	  rray never writes to its inputs.
147	
148	- `KEEP()` does not change refcounts, so borrowing never makes data look
149	  shared.
150	
151	Outside rray, other packages and base R go through the official API. They pay
152	at most one copy per wrapper, and then get a pointer that stays valid.
153	
154	## Rules for `x_data`
155	
156	These hold everywhere in `src/`.
157	
158	1. Name the unwrapped object `<name>_data`: `x_data`, `y_data`,
159	   `value_data`, `index_data`, `n_data`, `true_data`, and so on. Never
160	   `data`.
161	
162	2. Always `KEEP()` the result of `r_unwrap()`, even when it looks reachable
163	   through `x`.
164	
165	3. Read only data from `x_data`: `r_*_cbegin()`, `r_length()`,
166	   `r_typeof()`. Never read attributes from it. The wrapped vector still
167	   carries its own original attributes, which can be out of date. Dimensions
168	   and names always come from `x`.
169	
170	4. Never store `x_data` in an R object: never return it, never
171	   `r_list_poke()` it, never set it as an attribute. That would raise its
172	   refcount for good (refcounts do not go down when a list is garbage
173	   collected), and a locked wrapper's data must keep a refcount of 1.
174	
175	5. Never write through `x_data`. Only `r_*_cbegin()` on it, never
176	   `r_*_begin()`.
177	
178	## What gets unwrapped
179	
180	Every `r_<type>_cbegin()` on an object that is often our own wrapper, or that
181	can be as long as an array input:
182	
183	- Array payloads: `x`, `y`, `condition`, `true_`, `false_`, `missing`,
184	  `value`, the elements of `xs`.
185	
186	- Subscripts and index arrays.
187	
188	- `n` in `rray_roll_each()`, which goes through `rray_cast()` and
189	  `rray_broadcast()`.
190	
191	Out of scope, with the reason:
192	
193	- Dimensions, axes, and small integer arguments (`times` and `n` in
194	  `rray_rep()` and `rray_roll()`, `times` in `rray_rep_each()`, `dimensions`
195	  in `rray_split()`). These go through `arg_as_bare_integer()` and friends,
196	  which never wrap, so they are only wrappers if a user passes an rray result
197	  as the argument. Even then the cost is one copy of a short vector, after
198	  which that wrapper is locked and reads are free.
199	
200	- Names and titles (`r_list_cbegin(x_names)`, `r_chr_cbegin(axis_names)`).

+}
+expect_wraps <- function(w, x) {
+  expect_identical(wrapper_data_address(w), obj_address(x))
+}
+expect_not_wraps <- function(w, x) {
+  expect_identical(wrapper_data_address(w) == obj_address(x), FALSE)
+}
+```
+
+## Existing tests to update
+
+In `tests/testthat/test-wrapper.R`:
+
+- `expect_identical_addresses(wrapper_readonly(w), x)` becomes
+  `expect_wraps(w, x)`.
+
+- `expect_different_addresses(wrapper_readonly(w), x)` becomes
+  `expect_not_wraps(w, x)`.
+
+- `obj_address(wrapper_readonly(w))` becomes `wrapper_data_address(w)`.
+
+- The Part 3 test "unwrapping does not take ownership" becomes
+  `expect_wraps(w, x)`.

The plan is committed and pushed to fix/wrap as plans/wrap.md (commit 9172a64). The Codex review with the Sol model (gpt-6-sol) is running in the background, and I'll report what it finds when it finishes.

The plan has three stacked PRs:

  1. r_unwrap(). This adds the complement to r_wrap() and moves every array data read in src/ onto it. The C functions that do the reading get their parameters renamed to x_data, y_data, value_data and so on. Behaviour doesn't change.

  2. The lock. This ports base R's fix into src/wrapper.c. The first data pointer request copies shared data and locks the wrapper, and after that data1 never changes. A sweep test calls every exported function with wrapper inputs and checks that none of them gets locked, which proves PR 1 didn't miss a read.

  3. Owned results. This adds an ownership enum modelled on vctrs. rray_unstack() and rray_stack() then set attributes directly on arrays they just allocated, instead of wrapping them.

Decisions I made that you may want to challenge:

  • Dataptr_or_null returns NULL until the wrapper is locked. Base R copies there instead. R calls it from ITERATE_BY_REGION, so copying would make cheap region reads expensive.

  • Small arguments and names aren't unwrapped. This covers dimensions, axes, times, names and titles. We never wrap these ourselves, and the worst case is one copy of a short vector.

  • A locked wrapper that becomes shared is an internal error. This is the safety net for the rule that unwrapped data never ends up inside an R object.

  • Subscripts hold the unwrapped data. The subscript constructors unwrap, so the readers in extract.c and slice.c need no changes. The one helper that hands a subscript back to R clones it first.

  • Stale comments are listed, not edited. Following AGENTS.md, the agent leaves outdated comments in wrapper.c alone and lists them in the PR description for you to update.

I made the branch, the commit and the push only because you asked for them here. The commit message has no attribution line, as AGENTS.md requires.

void R_RunExitFinalizers(void)
{
    SEXP s;

    R_checkConstants(TRUE);

    for (s = R_weak_refs; s != R_NilValue; s = WEAKREF_NEXT(s))
	if (FINALIZE_ON_EXIT(s))
	    SET_READY_TO_FINALIZE(s);
    RunFinalizers();
}

void R_RunPendingFinalizers(void)
{
    if (R_finalizers_pending)
	RunFinalizers();
}

void R_RegisterFinalizerEx(SEXP s, SEXP fun, Rboolean onexit)
{
    R_MakeWeakRef(s, R_NilValue, fun, onexit);
}

/* "gc" a mark-sweep or in-place generational garbage collector */

void R_gc(void)
{
    num_old_gens_to_collect = NUM_OLD_GENERATIONS;
    R_gc_internal(0);
#ifndef IMMEDIATE_FINALIZERS
    R_RunPendingFinalizers();
#endif
}

void R_gc_lite(void)
{
    R_gc_internal(0);
#ifndef IMMEDIATE_FINALIZERS
    R_RunPendingFinalizers();
#endif
}

	   There is a small chance that running finalizers here may
	   chew up enough memory to make another immediate collection

Claude

I've finished part 1 of the plan (slice names). The work is committed as 8f05cc8 on a new branch, feature/slice-titles, which is pushed to origin. The full test suite passes.

The code change. rray_slice_names() in src/slice.c now takes x_names instead of v_x_names and copies the titles of x onto the names list it builds. Every function in the step goes through rray_slice(), so this one change covers them all: rray_slice(), rray_slice_axis(), rray_slice_rows(), rray_slice_columns(), rray_reverse(), rray_roll(), rray_rep() and rray_rep_each().

x <- array(1:24, c(2, 3, 4), dimnames = list(A = c("a", "b"), B = NULL, C = NULL))
rray_titles(rray_slice(x, 2L, c(3L, NA), TRUE))
#> [1] "A" "B" "C"

Tests. I added a titles test for each of those eight functions, placed next to their existing names tests in test-slice.R, test-reverse.R, test-roll.R and test-rep.R. Each one checks the plan's two inputs:

  • x, which has names and titles: the titles are kept.

  • y, which has titles but no names: the result's dimnames are identical to dimnames(y). Without the fix this case comes back as an unnamed list(NULL, NULL, NULL).

In test-slice.R the tests for the axis, rows and columns versions say the function's name, like the other tests in those sections.

Docs. I added "The titles of x are kept." next to the existing sentence about names in the docs for rray_slice(), rray_reverse(), rray_roll() and rray_rep(), then re-documented.

Protection check. Both x_names and its titles are reachable from x, which rray_slice() keeps protected. out is protected before the titles are poked onto it. When x has no titles the poke sets names to NULL, which allocates nothing.

Plan and commit. Step 1 is checked off in plans/titles.md. The commit message is the single sentence "Keep titles in rray_slice() and the functions built on it", with no co-author line, as AGENTS.md asks.

I didn't open a pull request.
